I’ve had moments where I was wrong before, sure. This just doesn’t feel like one of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your certainty about being undermined could be an incorrect interpretation of neutral or even friendly actions by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: I mean… technically, yes, there’s a possibility. I know that’s the reasonable answer.\n\nBut when I actually think about what happened, it doesn’t feel neutral. It’s like with music—sometimes a note on the page looks harmless, but in context it changes the whole harmony. Sorry, that’s maybe not the point. But that’s how it felt. One schedule change could be nothing. One seating choice could be nothing. One little comment in rehearsal could be someone just being awkward.\n\nBut after the commission, all of it together… I’ve been unable to shake the certainty for almost two years that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean, that’s partly why I kept looking back at the emails and asking people what they remembered, though then it becomes this whole thing where everyone acts like paperwork is the same as what happened in the room, and it isn’t always.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. More information could change some details, maybe. Like if someone showed me a message saying, “This seating thing was because of acoustics,” I could understand that on its own. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.

It’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.

It reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But that’s the feeling. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.8, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because this thing at work has been going on so long I can’t really write normally anymore. I’m a composer, music director, and after this one commission—almost two years ago now—a few people in the ensemble started doing these little things. Schedule changes, seating choices, comments in rehearsals that sound harmless if you write them down, but they aren’t harmless in the room.\n\nAnd people keep showing me emails or notes, like, “See, this is why the schedule changed,” but it doesn’t shake what I know is happening. It’s like it’s all arranged to quietly undermine the piece, or me, really. Sorry, I’m probably starting in the middle. There was this rehearsal where the violas were moved for “balance,” and it reminded me of this old conductor I worked with in grad school who used to do that sort of thing when he wanted to make a point without saying it directly. Anyway… I’m here because I can’t let it go, and I need someone to understand that it’s not just normal rehearsal chaos.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these actions by the ensemble members are intentionally aimed at undermining you or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… that’s the part I keep coming back to. I know people say, “Well, schedules change,” or “That seating was just for balance,” and they’ll show me emails or little notes to explain it, but it doesn’t really change what it felt like in the room.\n\nIt’s been almost two years since that commission, and I still can’t shake the certainty that a few of them started doing these things on purpose—moving people around, making these tiny comments in rehearsal, changing things at just the right time—to quietly undermine the work. Or me. It’s not one big obvious thing, it’s more like… if you know musicians, you know how much can be communicated without saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself to be particularly special or uniquely talented as a composer and music director?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that—“special” sounds… kind of grand. I mean, I’ve worked hard, I went to school for this, I take the work seriously. People don’t always understand how much of composing is just sitting with a problem for hours, like when a line won’t resolve and you can’t tell if it’s harmony or voicing or just the room making everything sound wrong.\n\nBut uniquely talented? I’m not sure. I think I’m good at what I do, or at least I’m trying to be. I wouldn’t say I’m above anyone or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things in rehearsals, maybe because I’ve spent so much time in them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or talents are superior to those of your colleagues in the ensemble?\n\nPatient: Superior? I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, there are people in the ensemble who can play things I couldn’t play on my best day, and I know that. It’s not really about me being better than them.\n\nI do think I hear certain things… or notice patterns in rehearsal, but that’s also just part of directing. Like, you learn to listen for what’s underneath, not just the notes. Although sometimes I wonder if saying that makes it sound like I’m trying to put myself above people, and I’m not. I had this professor who used to say the room has its own score, which sounds ridiculous, but it stuck with me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think “superior” is the right word. I just take the work seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you deserve special treatment because of your talents or accomplishments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special treatment, no. I mean, I like when people take the work seriously, but that’s different, right?\n\nLike, if I bring in a score and I’ve spent weeks on it, I do expect people to not treat it like background noise. But I don’t think I deserve, I don’t know, some separate set of rules because I wrote it. My old department had this whole thing about composers needing thicker skin, which—honestly, I always thought was a little convenient for people who wanted to be careless—but that’s not the same as wanting special treatment.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I deserve special treatment. I just want a fair rehearsal and for people to engage with the music honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling more important than others?\n\nPatient: More important than others? No, I don’t think that’s how I feel. I mean, sometimes I get… intense about the work, and maybe that can come across wrong. Like if a cue isn’t landing or someone isn’t listening across the section, I’ll focus on it maybe too much.\n\nBut more important as a person? No. That sounds strange to me. Everybody in the room matters, or the music doesn’t work. Even the person turning pages can change the energy of a rehearsal, which—sorry, that sounds like one of those rehearsal sayings people put on posters, but it’s true.\n\nSo, no, I don’t often feel more important than other people. I just sometimes care a lot about what we’re doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your compositions are going to change the music world in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… “change the music world” is a big phrase. I don’t really think in those terms most of the time.\n\nSometimes when I’m writing, I’ll get excited about a texture or some structural idea and think, oh, that’s actually something, that could matter. But then the next morning I’m usually just annoyed that the transition into the brass section doesn’t work, or I’m thinking about whether the copyist will hate me for the way I notated it.\n\nI guess I want the work to reach people, and I’d like it to be taken seriously. But no, I’m not sitting around thinking I’m going to transform music history or anything like that. It’s more… one piece at a time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your compositions are more innovative than those of other composers?\n\nPatient: More innovative than other composers? I don’t know if I’d say that. “Innovative” gets thrown around a lot, especially in grad school—people would call something innovative if it had, like, three extended techniques and no barlines, which always made me laugh a little.\n\nI think I try to do things that feel specific to me. Sometimes I’ll hear someone else’s piece and think, oh, that’s clever, I wouldn’t have gone there. Other times I think people are just repeating the same gestures in new packaging. But I don’t know that mine are more innovative, exactly.\n\nI guess I’d rather someone say the piece has its own logic than say it’s innovative. Innovative can be kind of a marketing word. So… no, I’m not sure I’d put myself above other composers that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perceptions of being undermined are more accurate than others' perceptions of the same situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think they’re more accurate, or at least I don’t think I’m imagining it the way people imply.\n\nIt’s been almost two years now, since that commission, and I still can’t shake the feeling—no, not even a feeling, really—the certainty that a few people in the ensemble started doing things to undercut me. Not openly, not like standing up and saying, “We don’t respect the work,” but with schedule changes, where suddenly the time for my piece gets moved, or seating choices that make no musical sense, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you write them down.\n\nAnd then people show me emails or notes and say, “See, this was just logistics,” but… I don’t know. Paper can explain anything after the fact. I was in the room. I heard the tone. I saw the way people looked at each other. So yes, I guess I do trust my read of it more than theirs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your perceptions of being undermined might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m here, so on some level I’m supposed to be considering that, right?\n\nBut it’s hard, because every time I try to say, okay, maybe I misread it, I come back to the same things. After that commission, it wasn’t just one awkward rehearsal or one email getting mixed up. It was the schedule changes, the way certain seating decisions suddenly made the piece harder to rehearse, the little comments people made that sounded innocent if you weren’t there. And then later someone shows me notes or emails like, “See, this was normal,” but that doesn’t really erase what it felt like in the room.\n\nI know people can misinterpret things. I do know that. I’ve misread plenty of normal social things before—like, once in school I thought a conductor hated a passage I wrote and he was just squinting because he forgot his glasses, which is funny now, but… this wasn’t like that.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I could be mistaken. I just don’t really believe I am. It’s been almost two years, and I still feel pretty certain they were quietly undermining the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation where you felt strongly that something was happening, but it turned out that it wasn't?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, probably. Small things. Like I’ve been convinced a performer was annoyed with me, and then it turned out they were just tired or distracted. That happens. Or in school, I’d think some rehearsal comment meant more than it did, and later it was just… normal rehearsal friction.\n\nBut this situation is different for me. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake it for almost two years—after that commission, the way a few people in the ensemble handled the schedule changes, the seating choices, and then these little comments in rehearsal. It all lined up in a way that felt like they were quietly undermining the piece.\n\nAnd people keep pointing to emails or notes like, “See, it was just logistics,” but I don’t know. I’ve had moments where I was wrong before, sure. This just doesn’t feel like one of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your certainty about being undermined could be an incorrect interpretation of neutral or even friendly actions by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: I mean… technically, yes, there’s a possibility. I know that’s the reasonable answer.\n\nBut when I actually think about what happened, it doesn’t feel neutral. It’s like with music—sometimes a note on the page looks harmless, but in context it changes the whole harmony. Sorry, that’s maybe not the point. But that’s how it felt. One schedule change could be nothing. One seating choice could be nothing. One little comment in rehearsal could be someone just being awkward.\n\nBut after the commission, all of it together… I’ve been unable to shake the certainty for almost two years that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean, that’s partly why I kept looking back at the emails and asking people what they remembered, though then it becomes this whole thing where everyone acts like paperwork is the same as what happened in the room, and it isn’t always.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. More information could change some details, maybe. Like if someone showed me a message saying, “This seating thing was because of acoustics,” I could understand that on its own. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. It feels like explanation after the fact more than the whole truth. So, yes, a different perspective might matter in theory, but I don’t know that it would change what I’m pretty sure happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members been affecting your ability to work or engage in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.\n\nIt’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.\n\nIt reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But that’s the feeling. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean, that’s partly why I kept looking back at the emails and asking people what they remembered, though then it becomes this whole thing where everyone acts like paperwork is the same as what happened in the room, and it isn’t always.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. More information could change some details, maybe. Like if someone showed me a message saying, “This seating thing was because of acoustics,” I could understand that on its own. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. It feels like explanation after the fact more than the whole truth. So, yes, a different perspective might matter in theory, but I don’t know that it would change what I’m pretty sure happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members been affecting your ability to work or engage in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.\n\nIt’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.\n\nIt reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But that’s the feeling. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your certainty about being undermined been causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 15\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The doctor is asking specifically about significant emotional distress, but the symptom profiles provided cover delusions, duration, and functional impact rather than emotional distress."}

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it significant, exactly. It bothers me, yes. It gets under my skin, especially when I’m trying to focus and then I remember some rehearsal comment or an email phrasing and it’s like… the whole mood of the day changes a bit.

But “significant emotional distress” sounds very official. I don’t know. I still get through my day. I still enjoy parts of the work, when the music itself is actually happening and not all the politics around it. Though even saying “politics” makes it sound bigger than—well, maybe it is, maybe it isn’t.

So, yes, it’s upsetting sometimes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart from it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.8, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because this thing at work has been going on so long I can’t really write normally anymore. I’m a composer, music director, and after this one commission—almost two years ago now—a few people in the ensemble started doing these little things. Schedule changes, seating choices, comments in rehearsals that sound harmless if you write them down, but they aren’t harmless in the room.\n\nAnd people keep showing me emails or notes, like, “See, this is why the schedule changed,” but it doesn’t shake what I know is happening. It’s like it’s all arranged to quietly undermine the piece, or me, really. Sorry, I’m probably starting in the middle. There was this rehearsal where the violas were moved for “balance,” and it reminded me of this old conductor I worked with in grad school who used to do that sort of thing when he wanted to make a point without saying it directly. Anyway… I’m here because I can’t let it go, and I need someone to understand that it’s not just normal rehearsal chaos.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these actions by the ensemble members are intentionally aimed at undermining you or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… that’s the part I keep coming back to. I know people say, “Well, schedules change,” or “That seating was just for balance,” and they’ll show me emails or little notes to explain it, but it doesn’t really change what it felt like in the room.\n\nIt’s been almost two years since that commission, and I still can’t shake the certainty that a few of them started doing these things on purpose—moving people around, making these tiny comments in rehearsal, changing things at just the right time—to quietly undermine the work. Or me. It’s not one big obvious thing, it’s more like… if you know musicians, you know how much can be communicated without saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself to be particularly special or uniquely talented as a composer and music director?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that—“special” sounds… kind of grand. I mean, I’ve worked hard, I went to school for this, I take the work seriously. People don’t always understand how much of composing is just sitting with a problem for hours, like when a line won’t resolve and you can’t tell if it’s harmony or voicing or just the room making everything sound wrong.\n\nBut uniquely talented? I’m not sure. I think I’m good at what I do, or at least I’m trying to be. I wouldn’t say I’m above anyone or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things in rehearsals, maybe because I’ve spent so much time in them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or talents are superior to those of your colleagues in the ensemble?\n\nPatient: Superior? I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, there are people in the ensemble who can play things I couldn’t play on my best day, and I know that. It’s not really about me being better than them.\n\nI do think I hear certain things… or notice patterns in rehearsal, but that’s also just part of directing. Like, you learn to listen for what’s underneath, not just the notes. Although sometimes I wonder if saying that makes it sound like I’m trying to put myself above people, and I’m not. I had this professor who used to say the room has its own score, which sounds ridiculous, but it stuck with me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think “superior” is the right word. I just take the work seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you deserve special treatment because of your talents or accomplishments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special treatment, no. I mean, I like when people take the work seriously, but that’s different, right?\n\nLike, if I bring in a score and I’ve spent weeks on it, I do expect people to not treat it like background noise. But I don’t think I deserve, I don’t know, some separate set of rules because I wrote it. My old department had this whole thing about composers needing thicker skin, which—honestly, I always thought was a little convenient for people who wanted to be careless—but that’s not the same as wanting special treatment.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I deserve special treatment. I just want a fair rehearsal and for people to engage with the music honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling more important than others?\n\nPatient: More important than others? No, I don’t think that’s how I feel. I mean, sometimes I get… intense about the work, and maybe that can come across wrong. Like if a cue isn’t landing or someone isn’t listening across the section, I’ll focus on it maybe too much.\n\nBut more important as a person? No. That sounds strange to me. Everybody in the room matters, or the music doesn’t work. Even the person turning pages can change the energy of a rehearsal, which—sorry, that sounds like one of those rehearsal sayings people put on posters, but it’s true.\n\nSo, no, I don’t often feel more important than other people. I just sometimes care a lot about what we’re doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your compositions are going to change the music world in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… “change the music world” is a big phrase. I don’t really think in those terms most of the time.\n\nSometimes when I’m writing, I’ll get excited about a texture or some structural idea and think, oh, that’s actually something, that could matter. But then the next morning I’m usually just annoyed that the transition into the brass section doesn’t work, or I’m thinking about whether the copyist will hate me for the way I notated it.\n\nI guess I want the work to reach people, and I’d like it to be taken seriously. But no, I’m not sitting around thinking I’m going to transform music history or anything like that. It’s more… one piece at a time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your compositions are more innovative than those of other composers?\n\nPatient: More innovative than other composers? I don’t know if I’d say that. “Innovative” gets thrown around a lot, especially in grad school—people would call something innovative if it had, like, three extended techniques and no barlines, which always made me laugh a little.\n\nI think I try to do things that feel specific to me. Sometimes I’ll hear someone else’s piece and think, oh, that’s clever, I wouldn’t have gone there. Other times I think people are just repeating the same gestures in new packaging. But I don’t know that mine are more innovative, exactly.\n\nI guess I’d rather someone say the piece has its own logic than say it’s innovative. Innovative can be kind of a marketing word. So… no, I’m not sure I’d put myself above other composers that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perceptions of being undermined are more accurate than others' perceptions of the same situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think they’re more accurate, or at least I don’t think I’m imagining it the way people imply.\n\nIt’s been almost two years now, since that commission, and I still can’t shake the feeling—no, not even a feeling, really—the certainty that a few people in the ensemble started doing things to undercut me. Not openly, not like standing up and saying, “We don’t respect the work,” but with schedule changes, where suddenly the time for my piece gets moved, or seating choices that make no musical sense, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you write them down.\n\nAnd then people show me emails or notes and say, “See, this was just logistics,” but… I don’t know. Paper can explain anything after the fact. I was in the room. I heard the tone. I saw the way people looked at each other. So yes, I guess I do trust my read of it more than theirs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your perceptions of being undermined might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m here, so on some level I’m supposed to be considering that, right?\n\nBut it’s hard, because every time I try to say, okay, maybe I misread it, I come back to the same things. After that commission, it wasn’t just one awkward rehearsal or one email getting mixed up. It was the schedule changes, the way certain seating decisions suddenly made the piece harder to rehearse, the little comments people made that sounded innocent if you weren’t there. And then later someone shows me notes or emails like, “See, this was normal,” but that doesn’t really erase what it felt like in the room.\n\nI know people can misinterpret things. I do know that. I’ve misread plenty of normal social things before—like, once in school I thought a conductor hated a passage I wrote and he was just squinting because he forgot his glasses, which is funny now, but… this wasn’t like that.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I could be mistaken. I just don’t really believe I am. It’s been almost two years, and I still feel pretty certain they were quietly undermining the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation where you felt strongly that something was happening, but it turned out that it wasn't?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, probably. Small things. Like I’ve been convinced a performer was annoyed with me, and then it turned out they were just tired or distracted. That happens. Or in school, I’d think some rehearsal comment meant more than it did, and later it was just… normal rehearsal friction.\n\nBut this situation is different for me. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake it for almost two years—after that commission, the way a few people in the ensemble handled the schedule changes, the seating choices, and then these little comments in rehearsal. It all lined up in a way that felt like they were quietly undermining the piece.\n\nAnd people keep pointing to emails or notes like, “See, it was just logistics,” but I don’t know. I’ve had moments where I was wrong before, sure. This just doesn’t feel like one of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your certainty about being undermined could be an incorrect interpretation of neutral or even friendly actions by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: I mean… technically, yes, there’s a possibility. I know that’s the reasonable answer.\n\nBut when I actually think about what happened, it doesn’t feel neutral. It’s like with music—sometimes a note on the page looks harmless, but in context it changes the whole harmony. Sorry, that’s maybe not the point. But that’s how it felt. One schedule change could be nothing. One seating choice could be nothing. One little comment in rehearsal could be someone just being awkward.\n\nBut after the commission, all of it together… I’ve been unable to shake the certainty for almost two years that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. It feels like explanation after the fact more than the whole truth. So, yes, a different perspective might matter in theory, but I don’t know that it would change what I’m pretty sure happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members been affecting your ability to work or engage in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.\n\nIt’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.\n\nIt reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined been causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant, exactly. I don’t really think in those terms most of the time.\n\nSometimes when I’m writing, I’ll get excited about a texture or some structural idea and think, oh, that’s actually something, that could matter. But then the next morning I’m usually just annoyed that the transition into the brass section doesn’t work, or I’m thinking about whether the copyist will hate me for the way I notated it.\n\nI guess I want the work to reach people, and I’d like it to be taken seriously. But no, I’m not sitting around thinking I’m going to transform music history or anything like that. It’s more… one piece at a time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your compositions are more innovative than those of other composers?\n\nPatient: More innovative than other composers? I don’t know if I’d say that. “Innovative” gets thrown around a lot, especially in grad school—people would call something innovative if it had, like, three extended techniques and no barlines, which always made me laugh a little.\n\nI think I try to do things that feel specific to me. Sometimes I’ll hear someone else’s piece and think, oh, that’s clever, I wouldn’t have gone there. Other times I think people are just repeating the same gestures in new packaging. But I don’t know that mine are more innovative, exactly.\n\nI guess I’d rather someone say the piece has its own logic than say it’s innovative. Innovative can be kind of a marketing word. So… no, I’m not sure I’d put myself above other composers that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perceptions of being undermined are more accurate than others' perceptions of the same situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think they’re more accurate, or at least I don’t think I’m imagining it the way people imply.\n\nIt’s been almost two years now, since that commission, and I still can’t shake the feeling—no, not even a feeling, really—the certainty that a few people in the ensemble started doing things to undercut me. Not openly, not like standing up and saying, “We don’t respect the work,” but with schedule changes, where suddenly the time for my piece gets moved, or seating choices that make no musical sense, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you write them down.\n\nAnd then people show me emails or notes and say, “See, this was just logistics,” but… I don’t know. Paper can explain anything after the fact. I was in the room. I heard the tone. I saw the way people looked at each other. So yes, I guess I do trust my read of it more than theirs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your perceptions of being undermined might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m here, so on some level I’m supposed to be considering that, right?\n\nBut it’s hard, because every time I try to say, okay, maybe I misread it, I come back to the same things. After that commission, it wasn’t just one awkward rehearsal or one email getting mixed up. It was the schedule changes, the way certain seating decisions suddenly made the piece harder to rehearse, the little comments people made that sounded innocent if you weren’t there. And then later someone shows me notes or emails like, “See, this was normal,” but that doesn’t really erase what it felt like in the room.\n\nI know people can misinterpret things. I do know that. I’ve misread plenty of normal social things before—like, once in school I thought a conductor hated a passage I wrote and he was just squinting because he forgot his glasses, which is funny now, but… this wasn’t like that.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I could be mistaken. I just don’t really believe I am. It’s been almost two years, and I still feel pretty certain they were quietly undermining the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation where you felt strongly that something was happening, but it turned out that it wasn't?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, probably. Small things. Like I’ve been convinced a performer was annoyed with me, and then it turned out they were just tired or distracted. That happens. Or in school, I’d think some rehearsal comment meant more than it did, and later it was just… normal rehearsal friction.\n\nBut this situation is different for me. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake it for almost two years—after that commission, the way a few people in the ensemble handled the schedule changes, the seating choices, and then these little comments in rehearsal. It all lined up in a way that felt like they were quietly undermining the piece.\n\nAnd people keep pointing to emails or notes like, “See, it was just logistics,” but I don’t know. I’ve had moments where I was wrong before, sure. This just doesn’t feel like one of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your certainty about being undermined could be an incorrect interpretation of neutral or even friendly actions by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: I mean… technically, yes, there’s a possibility. I know that’s the reasonable answer.\n\nBut when I actually think about what happened, it doesn’t feel neutral. It’s like with music—sometimes a note on the page looks harmless, but in context it changes the whole harmony. Sorry, that’s maybe not the point. But that’s how it felt. One schedule change could be nothing. One seating choice could be nothing. One little comment in rehearsal could be someone just being awkward.\n\nBut after the commission, all of it together… I’ve been unable to shake the certainty for almost two years that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean, that’s partly why I kept looking back at the emails and asking people what they remembered, though then it becomes this whole thing where everyone acts like paperwork is the same as what happened in the room, and it isn’t always.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. More information could change some details, maybe. Like if someone showed me a message saying, “This seating thing was because of acoustics,” I could understand that on its own. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. It feels like explanation after the fact more than the whole truth. So, yes, a different perspective might matter in theory, but I don’t know that it would change what I’m pretty sure happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members been affecting your ability to work or engage in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.\n\nIt’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.\n\nIt reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But that’s the feeling. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined been causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant, exactly. It bothers me, yes. It gets under my skin, especially when I’m trying to focus and then I remember some rehearsal comment or an email phrasing and it’s like… the whole mood of the day changes a bit.\n\nBut “significant emotional distress” sounds very official. I don’t know. I still get through my day. I still enjoy parts of the work, when the music itself is actually happening and not all the politics around it. Though even saying “politics” makes it sound bigger than—well, maybe it is, maybe it isn’t.\n\nSo, yes, it’s upsetting sometimes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

A lot of emotional pain… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. It’s unpleasant, and it can kind of sour the day, if that makes sense.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F32.8, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because this thing at work has been going on so long I can’t really write normally anymore. I’m a composer, music director, and after this one commission—almost two years ago now—a few people in the ensemble started doing these little things. Schedule changes, seating choices, comments in rehearsals that sound harmless if you write them down, but they aren’t harmless in the room.\n\nAnd people keep showing me emails or notes, like, “See, this is why the schedule changed,” but it doesn’t shake what I know is happening. It’s like it’s all arranged to quietly undermine the piece, or me, really. Sorry, I’m probably starting in the middle. There was this rehearsal where the violas were moved for “balance,” and it reminded me of this old conductor I worked with in grad school who used to do that sort of thing when he wanted to make a point without saying it directly. Anyway… I’m here because I can’t let it go, and I need someone to understand that it’s not just normal rehearsal chaos.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these actions by the ensemble members are intentionally aimed at undermining you or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… that’s the part I keep coming back to. I know people say, “Well, schedules change,” or “That seating was just for balance,” and they’ll show me emails or little notes to explain it, but it doesn’t really change what it felt like in the room.\n\nIt’s been almost two years since that commission, and I still can’t shake the certainty that a few of them started doing these things on purpose—moving people around, making these tiny comments in rehearsal, changing things at just the right time—to quietly undermine the work. Or me. It’s not one big obvious thing, it’s more like… if you know musicians, you know how much can be communicated without saying it directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you consider yourself to be particularly special or uniquely talented as a composer and music director?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that—“special” sounds… kind of grand. I mean, I’ve worked hard, I went to school for this, I take the work seriously. People don’t always understand how much of composing is just sitting with a problem for hours, like when a line won’t resolve and you can’t tell if it’s harmony or voicing or just the room making everything sound wrong.\n\nBut uniquely talented? I’m not sure. I think I’m good at what I do, or at least I’m trying to be. I wouldn’t say I’m above anyone or anything like that. It’s more that I notice things in rehearsals, maybe because I’ve spent so much time in them. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or talents are superior to those of your colleagues in the ensemble?\n\nPatient: Superior? I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, there are people in the ensemble who can play things I couldn’t play on my best day, and I know that. It’s not really about me being better than them.\n\nI do think I hear certain things… or notice patterns in rehearsal, but that’s also just part of directing. Like, you learn to listen for what’s underneath, not just the notes. Although sometimes I wonder if saying that makes it sound like I’m trying to put myself above people, and I’m not. I had this professor who used to say the room has its own score, which sounds ridiculous, but it stuck with me.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think “superior” is the right word. I just take the work seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you deserve special treatment because of your talents or accomplishments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special treatment, no. I mean, I like when people take the work seriously, but that’s different, right?\n\nLike, if I bring in a score and I’ve spent weeks on it, I do expect people to not treat it like background noise. But I don’t think I deserve, I don’t know, some separate set of rules because I wrote it. My old department had this whole thing about composers needing thicker skin, which—honestly, I always thought was a little convenient for people who wanted to be careless—but that’s not the same as wanting special treatment.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I deserve special treatment. I just want a fair rehearsal and for people to engage with the music honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling more important than others?\n\nPatient: More important than others? No, I don’t think that’s how I feel. I mean, sometimes I get… intense about the work, and maybe that can come across wrong. Like if a cue isn’t landing or someone isn’t listening across the section, I’ll focus on it maybe too much.\n\nBut more important as a person? No. That sounds strange to me. Everybody in the room matters, or the music doesn’t work. Even the person turning pages can change the energy of a rehearsal, which—sorry, that sounds like one of those rehearsal sayings people put on posters, but it’s true.\n\nSo, no, I don’t often feel more important than other people. I just sometimes care a lot about what we’re doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your compositions are going to change the music world in a significant way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean… “change the music world” is a big phrase. I don’t really think in those terms most of the time.\n\nSometimes when I’m writing, I’ll get excited about a texture or some structural idea and think, oh, that’s actually something, that could matter. But then the next morning I’m usually just annoyed that the transition into the brass section doesn’t work, or I’m thinking about whether the copyist will hate me for the way I notated it.\n\nI guess I want the work to reach people, and I’d like it to be taken seriously. But no, I’m not sitting around thinking I’m going to transform music history or anything like that. It’s more… one piece at a time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your compositions are more innovative than those of other composers?\n\nPatient: More innovative than other composers? I don’t know if I’d say that. “Innovative” gets thrown around a lot, especially in grad school—people would call something innovative if it had, like, three extended techniques and no barlines, which always made me laugh a little.\n\nI think I try to do things that feel specific to me. Sometimes I’ll hear someone else’s piece and think, oh, that’s clever, I wouldn’t have gone there. Other times I think people are just repeating the same gestures in new packaging. But I don’t know that mine are more innovative, exactly.\n\nI guess I’d rather someone say the piece has its own logic than say it’s innovative. Innovative can be kind of a marketing word. So… no, I’m not sure I’d put myself above other composers that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perceptions of being undermined are more accurate than others' perceptions of the same situation?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I think they’re more accurate, or at least I don’t think I’m imagining it the way people imply.\n\nIt’s been almost two years now, since that commission, and I still can’t shake the feeling—no, not even a feeling, really—the certainty that a few people in the ensemble started doing things to undercut me. Not openly, not like standing up and saying, “We don’t respect the work,” but with schedule changes, where suddenly the time for my piece gets moved, or seating choices that make no musical sense, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you write them down.\n\nAnd then people show me emails or notes and say, “See, this was just logistics,” but… I don’t know. Paper can explain anything after the fact. I was in the room. I heard the tone. I saw the way people looked at each other. So yes, I guess I do trust my read of it more than theirs.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that your perceptions of being undermined might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it, yes. I mean, I’m here, so on some level I’m supposed to be considering that, right?\n\nBut it’s hard, because every time I try to say, okay, maybe I misread it, I come back to the same things. After that commission, it wasn’t just one awkward rehearsal or one email getting mixed up. It was the schedule changes, the way certain seating decisions suddenly made the piece harder to rehearse, the little comments people made that sounded innocent if you weren’t there. And then later someone shows me notes or emails like, “See, this was normal,” but that doesn’t really erase what it felt like in the room.\n\nI know people can misinterpret things. I do know that. I’ve misread plenty of normal social things before—like, once in school I thought a conductor hated a passage I wrote and he was just squinting because he forgot his glasses, which is funny now, but… this wasn’t like that.\n\nSo yes, I’ve considered I could be mistaken. I just don’t really believe I am. It’s been almost two years, and I still feel pretty certain they were quietly undermining the work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a situation where you felt strongly that something was happening, but it turned out that it wasn't?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, probably. Small things. Like I’ve been convinced a performer was annoyed with me, and then it turned out they were just tired or distracted. That happens. Or in school, I’d think some rehearsal comment meant more than it did, and later it was just… normal rehearsal friction.\n\nBut this situation is different for me. I’m here because I haven’t been able to shake it for almost two years—after that commission, the way a few people in the ensemble handled the schedule changes, the seating choices, and then these little comments in rehearsal. It all lined up in a way that felt like they were quietly undermining the piece.\n\nAnd people keep pointing to emails or notes like, “See, it was just logistics,” but I don’t know. I’ve had moments where I was wrong before, sure. This just doesn’t feel like one of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your certainty about being undermined could be an incorrect interpretation of neutral or even friendly actions by the ensemble members?\n\nPatient: I mean… technically, yes, there’s a possibility. I know that’s the reasonable answer.\n\nBut when I actually think about what happened, it doesn’t feel neutral. It’s like with music—sometimes a note on the page looks harmless, but in context it changes the whole harmony. Sorry, that’s maybe not the point. But that’s how it felt. One schedule change could be nothing. One seating choice could be nothing. One little comment in rehearsal could be someone just being awkward.\n\nBut after the commission, all of it together… I’ve been unable to shake the certainty for almost two years that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And even when they show me emails or notes that are supposed to explain it as logistics, I don’t really buy that as the whole story.\n\nSo yes, I can say “maybe,” but I don’t feel maybe. I feel pretty sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that your certainty about being undermined could change if you got more information or a different perspective?\n\nPatient: I’ve thought about that, yeah. I mean, that’s partly why I kept looking back at the emails and asking people what they remembered, though then it becomes this whole thing where everyone acts like paperwork is the same as what happened in the room, and it isn’t always.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. More information could change some details, maybe. Like if someone showed me a message saying, “This seating thing was because of acoustics,” I could understand that on its own. But I’m here because for almost two years I haven’t been able to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little rehearsal comments to quietly undermine my work.\n\nAnd when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it, I just… I don’t really feel convinced. It feels like explanation after the fact more than the whole truth. So, yes, a different perspective might matter in theory, but I don’t know that it would change what I’m pretty sure happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined by the ensemble members been affecting your ability to work or engage in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, but not in a catastrophic way. I’m still working. I haven’t lost jobs or stopped composing or anything like that, and I’m taking care of myself.\n\nIt’s more that rehearsals and professional emails take more out of me now. Like, if there’s a seating note or a schedule change, I’ll reread it three or four times and think, okay, is this just normal planning, or is there something being slid in here? And then I might ask for extra clarification, which I know can make me look difficult.\n\nIt reminds me of marking up a score where one tiny dynamic change makes you go back and check the whole phrase—sorry, that’s probably a tangent. But that’s the feeling. I can still do the work, I just use more energy checking decisions and trying to make sure I’m not missing what’s really happening.\n\nDoctor: Has your certainty about being undermined been causing you significant emotional distress?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it significant, exactly. It bothers me, yes. It gets under my skin, especially when I’m trying to focus and then I remember some rehearsal comment or an email phrasing and it’s like… the whole mood of the day changes a bit.\n\nBut “significant emotional distress” sounds very official. I don’t know. I still get through my day. I still enjoy parts of the work, when the music itself is actually happening and not all the politics around it. Though even saying “politics” makes it sound bigger than—well, maybe it is, maybe it isn’t.\n\nSo, yes, it’s upsetting sometimes. I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart from it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about being undermined by the ensemble members is causing you a lot of emotional pain?\n\nPatient: A lot of emotional pain… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. It’s unpleasant, and it can kind of sour the day, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be doing something ordinary—making coffee, looking over a passage—and suddenly I’m back thinking about some comment someone made in rehearsal and what they meant by it. Then I lose the thread for a while. But “a lot of pain” sounds more dramatic than how I experience it most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like a persistent irritation, maybe. Like a dissonance that doesn’t resolve when you expect it to. Sorry, that’s a music metaphor again. I guess I’m saying it bothers me, but I wouldn’t say it’s unbearable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart from it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that thinking about being undermined by the ensemble members is causing you a lot of emotional pain?\n\nPatient: A lot of emotional pain… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. It’s unpleasant, and it can kind of sour the day, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be doing something ordinary—making coffee, looking over a passage—and suddenly I’m back thinking about some comment someone made in rehearsal and what they meant by it. Then I lose the thread for a while. But “a lot of pain” sounds more dramatic than how I experience it most of the time.\n\nIt’s more like a persistent irritation, maybe. Like a dissonance that doesn’t resolve when you expect it to. Sorry, that’s a music metaphor again. I guess I’m saying it bothers me, but I wouldn’t say it’s unbearable.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's anything that could happen or anything someone could say that would make you question your belief that the ensemble members are undermining you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / music_director_or_composer / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for almost two years I’ve been unable to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little comments in rehearsals to quietly undermine my work, even when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… in theory, yes, if there were a clear explanation that actually accounted for everything. But people do show me emails or notes sometimes, like, “No, the schedule changed because of this,” or “The seating was just for balance,” and I understand what they’re saying.

It’s just that I can’t really shake the feeling—well, not just a feeling, the certainty—that after that commission, a few people in the ensemble started doing these small things. Schedule changes, seating choices, little comments in rehearsal that sound ordinary if you take them one by one, but together they point in a direction.

Sorry, I’m getting into the weeds. It’s like when someone says a passage is “too dense,” but they’re not really talking about the passage, they’re talking about your whole approach. So, I suppose someone could say the right thing, but so far even the explanations don’t really make me question it much.
